========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.

And then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.

And then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.

I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.\n\nAnd then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.

It’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.

I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.\n\nAnd then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.

Then if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.

So yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.

I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on since the ladder accident about a year and seven months ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.

At first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.

And yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.

And the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.

I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.\n\nAnd then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.\n\nAnd yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"Mostly I'm here because I don't sleep right anymore; I lie there a long time, then wake up from bad dreams or little noises and can't seem to settle back down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.

I can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.

So even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF51.0, F41.1, G47.00, G47.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.\n\nAnd then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.\n\nAnd yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.\n\nAnd the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"Mostly I'm here because I don't sleep right anymore; I lie there a long time, then wake up from bad dreams or little noises and can't seem to settle back down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Even on a night where, if you looked at the clock, you might say I got enough hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep to me. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.

So in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.\n\nAnd the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.

Mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll have plenty of time, sure, like I’m in bed early enough and there’s no reason I shouldn’t sleep, but then I lie there a long time before I can drop off. And when I do, it doesn’t seem to last right. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a little noise will do it, and then I can’t seem to settle myself back down.

So if you mean a night where I fell asleep easy, stayed asleep fine, and still woke up unrefreshed… I can’t really say that’s the main thing. The main thing is I don’t get that smooth sleep to begin with. It’s broken up, and then morning comes and I feel like I never really got rested.

That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even on a night where, if you looked at the clock, you might say I got enough hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep to me. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a night where you felt like you had plenty of time to sleep and didn’t have any trouble falling or staying asleep, but still woke up feeling unrefreshed?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll have plenty of time, sure, like I’m in bed early enough and there’s no reason I shouldn’t sleep, but then I lie there a long time before I can drop off. And when I do, it doesn’t seem to last right. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a little noise will do it, and then I can’t seem to settle myself back down.\n\nSo if you mean a night where I fell asleep easy, stayed asleep fine, and still woke up unrefreshed… I can’t really say that’s the main thing. The main thing is I don’t get that smooth sleep to begin with. It’s broken up, and then morning comes and I feel like I never really got rested.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often wake up feeling just as tired as when you went to bed, even when you had the chance to sleep for a long time and didn’t wake up during the night?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess I’d say I do wake up tired a lot, yes, but not usually because I had some nice long stretch of sleep without waking. That’s the part that doesn’t really fit for me.

Mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll get in bed and I’ve got the time to sleep, I’m not staying up on purpose or anything, but I just lie there and lie there. Sometimes it feels like my body is tired but my mind won’t let go. Then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel solid. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, and once I’m awake it’s like I can’t get settled again.

So by morning, yes, I feel about as tired as I did when I went to bed—sometimes more—but it’s because the night gets broken up, not because I slept straight through and still felt bad.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF51.0, F41.1, G47.00, G47.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I mean, I go to bed and I’m tired, or I think I am, but then I just lay there… feels like forever sometimes. My mind doesn’t settle down the way it used to.\n\nAnd then if I do finally drift off, I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, something I probably wouldn’t have paid any attention to before. Once I’m awake, that’s it a lot of the time. I try turning over, closing my eyes, telling myself to calm down, but I can’t seem to get settled back down again. It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.\n\nAnd yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.\n\nAnd the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can really say that happens, not exactly like that. If I fall asleep easy and stay asleep the whole night, that would be unusual for me lately.

Mostly the trouble is that I don’t sleep right in the first place. I’ll get into bed and be there a long time, just lying there, tired but not able to drop off. Then when I finally do sleep, it’s not steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises, things that maybe shouldn’t wake a person up, but they do. And once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down again.

So when I wake up feeling like I didn’t get restful sleep, it’s usually because the night was like that—broken up, restless, like I never got all the way into a good sleep. I wouldn’t say it’s mostly happening after a smooth, easy night. It’s more that I hardly get those smooth nights anymore.

I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… if there really were no interruptions and I slept straight through, I’m not sure that’s what usually happens with me. I mean, I do wake up feeling like I didn’t get good rest, yes, but most times it’s because the sleep itself wasn’t good.

Mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I can have plenty of time in bed, I’m not shorting myself on purpose, but I’ll lie there a long time before I actually fall asleep. And then once I do, it’s like I don’t stay settled. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes it’s just some little noise that catches me, and then I’m awake and can’t seem to get myself calmed down enough to drift back off.

So in the morning I feel worn out, but I wouldn’t say it’s after a clean, easy night with no interruptions. It’s more that the night never feels smooth to begin with.

I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.\n\nAnd yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.\n\nAnd the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even on a night where, if you looked at the clock, you might say I got enough hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep to me. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a night where you felt like you had plenty of time to sleep and didn’t have any trouble falling or staying asleep, but still woke up feeling unrefreshed?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll have plenty of time, sure, like I’m in bed early enough and there’s no reason I shouldn’t sleep, but then I lie there a long time before I can drop off. And when I do, it doesn’t seem to last right. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a little noise will do it, and then I can’t seem to settle myself back down.\n\nSo if you mean a night where I fell asleep easy, stayed asleep fine, and still woke up unrefreshed… I can’t really say that’s the main thing. The main thing is I don’t get that smooth sleep to begin with. It’s broken up, and then morning comes and I feel like I never really got rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wake up feeling just as tired as when you went to bed, even when you had the chance to sleep for a long time and didn’t wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’d say I do wake up tired a lot, yes, but not usually because I had some nice long stretch of sleep without waking. That’s the part that doesn’t really fit for me.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll get in bed and I’ve got the time to sleep, I’m not staying up on purpose or anything, but I just lie there and lie there. Sometimes it feels like my body is tired but my mind won’t let go. Then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel solid. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, and once I’m awake it’s like I can’t get settled again.\n\nSo by morning, yes, I feel about as tired as I did when I went to bed—sometimes more—but it’s because the night gets broken up, not because I slept straight through and still felt bad.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you feel like you fell asleep easily and stayed asleep without waking up, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say that happens, not exactly like that. If I fall asleep easy and stay asleep the whole night, that would be unusual for me lately.\n\nMostly the trouble is that I don’t sleep right in the first place. I’ll get into bed and be there a long time, just lying there, tired but not able to drop off. Then when I finally do sleep, it’s not steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises, things that maybe shouldn’t wake a person up, but they do. And once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down again.\n\nSo when I wake up feeling like I didn’t get restful sleep, it’s usually because the night was like that—broken up, restless, like I never got all the way into a good sleep. I wouldn’t say it’s mostly happening after a smooth, easy night. It’s more that I hardly get those smooth nights anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you have plenty of time in bed and no interruptions, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: Well… if there really were no interruptions and I slept straight through, I’m not sure that’s what usually happens with me. I mean, I do wake up feeling like I didn’t get good rest, yes, but most times it’s because the sleep itself wasn’t good.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I can have plenty of time in bed, I’m not shorting myself on purpose, but I’ll lie there a long time before I actually fall asleep. And then once I do, it’s like I don’t stay settled. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes it’s just some little noise that catches me, and then I’m awake and can’t seem to get myself calmed down enough to drift back off.\n\nSo in the morning I feel worn out, but I wouldn’t say it’s after a clean, easy night with no interruptions. It’s more that the night never feels smooth to begin with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s more that the night never feels smooth to begin with.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever notice any unusual sensations in your legs, like crawling or restlessness, especially when you're trying to fall asleep, or do you snore loudly or have pauses in your breathing during sleep?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. My legs don’t usually feel like bugs crawling or like I have to keep moving them when I’m trying to fall asleep. I mean, at my age I get aches here and there, but that’s not the same thing you’re asking, I don’t think.

It’s been wearing on me, so I figured I ought to come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more anxious or worried lately, especially around the time you’re trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I mostly notice it as not being able to sleep. I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body’s tired, but my head just won’t quiet down. I’ll lie there a long time, turning this way and that, waiting to drift off, and the longer I’m awake the more worked up I get about the fact that I’m still awake.\n\nAnd then when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just some little noise in the house, something I might’ve slept right through years ago. Once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down. I tell myself, “Just close your eyes, just rest,” but it doesn’t really take. So yes, around bedtime especially, I guess I do get anxious, because I’m already expecting it’s going to be another rough night.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless most days, even during the daytime when you’re not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say hopeless, no. I mean, I get worn down, sure, especially after a rough night, and I can be kind of low or quiet the next day because I’m tired and not myself. But if you mean am I sitting around all day feeling sad like there’s no point to anything… I don’t think that’s really it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m frustrated, I guess. Dragged out. Some days I don’t feel like doing much, but I wouldn’t say I’m down most days in that way. I haven’t really thought of it as sadness, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or unrefreshed after a full night’s sleep, or find yourself struggling to stay awake or alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel tired a lot in the daytime, but it’s hard for me to call it a “full night’s sleep,” because most nights don’t feel full to me. I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. I’ll turn over, close my eyes, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body doesn’t really go back to sleep the way it should.\n\nSo yes, during the day I can feel pretty worn out and unrefreshed. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. But it just sort of stayed. Some nights are worse than others, but the trouble falling asleep and then waking up and not getting back to sleep—that’s been going on since then.\n\nAnd yes, it can happen even when I don’t feel especially worked up about anything. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get tired in the day, like I was saying, but I don’t just suddenly fall asleep out of nowhere, not like sitting there one minute and then I’m gone without warning. If I’m tired, I usually know I’m tired.\n\nAnd the automatic thing… I’m trying to think. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even on a night where, if you looked at the clock, you might say I got enough hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep to me. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a night where you felt like you had plenty of time to sleep and didn’t have any trouble falling or staying asleep, but still woke up feeling unrefreshed?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a night where you felt like you had plenty of time to sleep and didn’t have any trouble falling or staying asleep, but still woke up feeling unrefreshed?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll have plenty of time, sure, like I’m in bed early enough and there’s no reason I shouldn’t sleep, but then I lie there a long time before I can drop off. And when I do, it doesn’t seem to last right. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a little noise will do it, and then I can’t seem to settle myself back down.\n\nSo if you mean a night where I fell asleep easy, stayed asleep fine, and still woke up unrefreshed… I can’t really say that’s the main thing. The main thing is I don’t get that smooth sleep to begin with. It’s broken up, and then morning comes and I feel like I never really got rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wake up feeling just as tired as when you went to bed, even when you had the chance to sleep for a long time and didn’t wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’d say I do wake up tired a lot, yes, but not usually because I had some nice long stretch of sleep without waking. That’s the part that doesn’t really fit for me.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll get in bed and I’ve got the time to sleep, I’m not staying up on purpose or anything, but I just lie there and lie there. Sometimes it feels like my body is tired but my mind won’t let go. Then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel solid. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, and once I’m awake it’s like I can’t get settled again.\n\nSo by morning, yes, I feel about as tired as I did when I went to bed—sometimes more—but it’s because the night gets broken up, not because I slept straight through and still felt bad.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you feel like you fell asleep easily and stayed asleep without waking up, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say that happens, not exactly like that. If I fall asleep easy and stay asleep the whole night, that would be unusual for me lately.\n\nMostly the trouble is that I don’t sleep right in the first place. I’ll get into bed and be there a long time, just lying there, tired but not able to drop off. Then when I finally do sleep, it’s not steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises, things that maybe shouldn’t wake a person up, but they do. And once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down again.\n\nSo when I wake up feeling like I didn’t get restful sleep, it’s usually because the night was like that—broken up, restless, like I never got all the way into a good sleep. I wouldn’t say it’s mostly happening after a smooth, easy night. It’s more that I hardly get those smooth nights anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you have plenty of time in bed and no interruptions, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: Well… if there really were no interruptions and I slept straight through, I’m not sure that’s what usually happens with me. I mean, I do wake up feeling like I didn’t get good rest, yes, but most times it’s because the sleep itself wasn’t good.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I can have plenty of time in bed, I’m not shorting myself on purpose, but I’ll lie there a long time before I actually fall asleep. And then once I do, it’s like I don’t stay settled. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes it’s just some little noise that catches me, and then I’m awake and can’t seem to get myself calmed down enough to drift back off.\n\nSo in the morning I feel worn out, but I wouldn’t say it’s after a clean, easy night with no interruptions. It’s more that the night never feels smooth to begin with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any unusual sensations in your legs, like crawling or restlessness, especially when you're trying to fall asleep, or do you snore loudly or have pauses in your breathing during sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. My legs don’t usually feel like bugs crawling or like I have to keep moving them when I’m trying to fall asleep. I mean, at my age I get aches here and there, but that’s not the same thing you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nAs far as snoring or stopping breathing, I live with myself, so it’s a little hard to say for sure. Nobody’s told me I stop breathing in my sleep, and I don’t know about loud snoring. I haven’t woken up thinking, “Oh, I couldn’t breathe,” or anything like that. I guess I’d put it this way… if I truly don’t wake up at all, then that’s not really the usual problem I’m having. Mostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore.

Most nights I’ll get into bed tired, and I think, well, I ought to be able to sleep now, but then I just lie there. Sometimes it feels like I’m lying there forever, looking at the dark, shifting around, trying not to think too much. Then when sleep finally does come, it doesn’t feel solid. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes it’s just a little noise that sets me off, something small, and then once I’m awake I can’t seem to settle myself back down.

So when I get up feeling like the sleep wasn’t deep or refreshing, it’s usually because of that whole pattern. It’s not so much that I sleep straight through and still feel unrested. It’s more that I don’t really get a good, steady night in the first place.

I’ll go to bed tired, thinking maybe tonight I’ll sleep, and then I’m just lying there for a long stretch. Sometimes I don’t even know how much time has passed, but it feels like I’m awake forever.\n\nThen if I finally do fall asleep, it’s not the kind of sleep where you wake up rested. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a small noise in the house, and once I’m up, I can’t seem to get settled back down. Not always like I’m going to fall asleep in a chair, exactly, but more like I’m dragging myself through the day because the night didn’t really give me any rest.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with falling asleep and staying asleep, and does it seem to happen even when you don’t feel particularly anxious or stressed?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, pretty much since that ladder accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now, if I’m counting it right. I didn’t used to have this kind of trouble the way I do now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of everything right after it happened, you know, like my body and my nerves were all stirred up, and I figured sleep would come back to normal once some time passed. I mean, sometimes I am tense because I’m already worried I won’t sleep, but other times I’ll think I’m having a fairly ordinary evening, nothing big on my mind, and I still get into bed and it starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself suddenly falling asleep during the day, even in situations where you’re not expecting to, or ever done something automatically without really being aware of it, like driving a short distance without remembering the drive?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had times where I’m distracted or I don’t remember every little turn I made, especially on a familiar road, but I wouldn’t say I’ve come to and realized I drove somewhere with no awareness of it. Nothing like that really stands out to me. If it’s happened, I haven’t noticed it in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up feeling as though you haven’t slept at all, even if you were in bed for a long time and think you may have slept?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here, because I don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nI can be in bed for hours, and I know I must have drifted off at some point, but when morning comes it feels like I didn’t really get sleep. It’s more like I was just lying there all night, half awake, half listening. I’ll spend a long time trying to fall asleep, and then when I finally do, something wakes me up—bad dreams sometimes, or even just some little noise—and after that I can’t get myself settled back down.\n\nSo even if I was in bed a long time, I don’t wake up feeling like, “All right, I slept.” I wake up worn out, like the night didn’t do what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unrefreshed in the morning even if you feel like you managed to sleep for a decent amount of time during the night?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even on a night where, if you looked at the clock, you might say I got enough hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep to me. I’ll go to bed and lie there a long while before I finally drift off, and then it’s like the sleep is so light it doesn’t hold. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or just from some small noise, and once I’m awake my mind and body won’t settle back down.\n\nSo in the morning, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I spent the whole night fighting to sleep instead of actually sleeping. That’s mostly why I’m here, really—I just don’t sleep right anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a night where you felt like you had plenty of time to sleep and didn’t have any trouble falling or staying asleep, but still woke up feeling unrefreshed?\n\nPatient: Well… I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Usually if I wake up feeling worn out, there was some trouble in the night, even if it doesn’t look like much from the outside.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll have plenty of time, sure, like I’m in bed early enough and there’s no reason I shouldn’t sleep, but then I lie there a long time before I can drop off. And when I do, it doesn’t seem to last right. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes just a little noise will do it, and then I can’t seem to settle myself back down.\n\nSo if you mean a night where I fell asleep easy, stayed asleep fine, and still woke up unrefreshed… I can’t really say that’s the main thing. The main thing is I don’t get that smooth sleep to begin with. It’s broken up, and then morning comes and I feel like I never really got rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wake up feeling just as tired as when you went to bed, even when you had the chance to sleep for a long time and didn’t wake up during the night?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’d say I do wake up tired a lot, yes, but not usually because I had some nice long stretch of sleep without waking. That’s the part that doesn’t really fit for me.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll get in bed and I’ve got the time to sleep, I’m not staying up on purpose or anything, but I just lie there and lie there. Sometimes it feels like my body is tired but my mind won’t let go. Then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel solid. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or even just some little noise in the house, and once I’m awake it’s like I can’t get settled again.\n\nSo by morning, yes, I feel about as tired as I did when I went to bed—sometimes more—but it’s because the night gets broken up, not because I slept straight through and still felt bad.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you feel like you fell asleep easily and stayed asleep without waking up, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say that happens, not exactly like that. If I fall asleep easy and stay asleep the whole night, that would be unusual for me lately.\n\nMostly the trouble is that I don’t sleep right in the first place. I’ll get into bed and be there a long time, just lying there, tired but not able to drop off. Then when I finally do sleep, it’s not steady. I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises, things that maybe shouldn’t wake a person up, but they do. And once I’m awake, I can’t seem to settle back down again.\n\nSo when I wake up feeling like I didn’t get restful sleep, it’s usually because the night was like that—broken up, restless, like I never got all the way into a good sleep. I wouldn’t say it’s mostly happening after a smooth, easy night. It’s more that I hardly get those smooth nights anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you have plenty of time in bed and no interruptions, do you still wake up feeling like you didn’t get restful sleep?\n\nPatient: Well… if there really were no interruptions and I slept straight through, I’m not sure that’s what usually happens with me. I mean, I do wake up feeling like I didn’t get good rest, yes, but most times it’s because the sleep itself wasn’t good.\n\nMostly I’m here because I just don’t sleep right anymore. I can have plenty of time in bed, I’m not shorting myself on purpose, but I’ll lie there a long time before I actually fall asleep. And then once I do, it’s like I don’t stay settled. I’ll wake up from bad dreams, or sometimes it’s just some little noise that catches me, and then I’m awake and can’t seem to get myself calmed down enough to drift back off.\n\nSo in the morning I feel worn out, but I wouldn’t say it’s after a clean, easy night with no interruptions. It’s more that the night never feels smooth to begin with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any unusual sensations in your legs, like crawling or restlessness, especially when you're trying to fall asleep, or do you snore loudly or have pauses in your breathing during sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. My legs don’t usually feel like bugs crawling or like I have to keep moving them when I’m trying to fall asleep. I mean, at my age I get aches here and there, but that’s not the same thing you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nAs far as snoring or stopping breathing, I live with myself, so it’s a little hard to say for sure. Nobody’s told me I stop breathing in my sleep, and I don’t know about loud snoring. I haven’t woken up thinking, “Oh, I couldn’t breathe,” or anything like that. So… no, I can’t really give you much there. I haven’t noticed those things.\n\nDoctor: Even on nights when you don’t wake up during the night, do you still wake up feeling like your sleep wasn’t deep or refreshing?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d put it this way… if I truly don’t wake up at all, then that’s not really the usual problem I’m having. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, there are nights where I don’t feel like I’m worked up about anything in particular. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight ought to be fine,” because nothing much happened, I’m not sitting there arguing with somebody in my head or anything like that.

But then I get in bed and it still doesn’t come easy. I just lie there a long time, wide awake, kind of waiting for sleep to happen. And the more I notice I’m not sleeping, the harder it gets. Then if I finally do fall asleep, it still doesn’t always stay that way. I might wake up from a bad dream, or just some small noise, and then I’m up again and can’t settle back down.

So yes, even when I start out feeling calm, my sleep can still go bad on me. It’s like my body doesn’t know how to just rest through the night anymore.
